ParlaMint-DK_20160315130038	Thank you. The last few weeks have revealed a serious problem which we were all well aware of, but which has rarely been so clearly identified in public as we have seen it. Wives who are urged to remain with a violent husband and who must accept multi-wifery; parents who learn to beat their children; women who are in some cases to turn down jobs if there are men at work; religious rules that are placed above the laws of society. It is now clear to everyone, perhaps even more clearly than we have seen in the past, that some around certain religious communities abuse our freedoms, religious freedom, freedom of expression, to undermine the very democracy on which freedoms are based and derived. And what do we do about it? If there was a very simple, easy solution, I am sure it was used long ago, but in the fight against anti-democratic and extremist environments there is unfortunately no finger-tip solution. We have set a series of things in motion in order to strengthen the preventive action we have together. We recently adopted two legislative proposals aimed at strengthening the tools of the authorities to prevent radicalisation, among others. the possibility of seclusion of extremist prisoners into prisons. We have set aside resources to give young men and women seeking a way out of closed radical environments an opportunity to do so through a new exit centre. We've tightened up PET's capabilities. We have stepped up our efforts against Syrian warriors, and the bill on treason was passed in the Folketing shortly before Christmas. So we have done a great deal, but we must also honestly say that we have not done enough. Unfortunately, the easy solutions are not there because we have something here that is a real paradox. How do you fight something that is protected by our fundamental values of society? It's hard. It hits right into the heart of our democracy, right into our social souls. It is a paradox that many of the solutions that have been put forward immediately run counter to the fact that this cannot be done because there are fundamental rights, and what are we going to do with those who use these fundamental rights to protect themselves behind them, to really try to break down our social model? I welcome the fact that over the last few weeks the political parties of the Folketing, which they are absolutely most, have been strongly opposed to the statements we have seen about, among other things. The ugly mosque. Many have also indicated a considerable willingness to tackle the problem, to try to resolve it, and I would like to sign for that, because in my view we are facing a challenge here, which is beyond general party politics, but which is really about how we, who fight peacefully in here, protect ourselves from those who basically want democracy to be put to death. We're gonna have a fight. I do not have the answers, not on a stand-by basis, but I would like to offer to invite those parties in this Chamber who acknowledge this paradox and who would like to do something about it so that we can give a united, effective response, not just shouting out our impotence from the rooftops, but making something that works into cooperation with the government. Therefore, immediately after Easter, I would like to invite the party leaders to a meeting of the Prime Minister's Office to launch a work with the church minister Bertel Haarder in the lead, together with the integration minister Inger Støjberg and other relevant ministers, to find solutions that work, that respect democracy, but at the same time take a hard stand against those who do not respect our laws, who say away from those who seek to limit our freedom, who are fighting against those who basically want a fight with the free society that we all know and love. It's a challenge that embraces widely. It is about our fundamental democratic values, our culture, our history, about the role of the municipalities, about education, about faith and about many, many other things. That is why I would like to invite and appeal to broad cooperation. What we have here is not a new problem. It existed under the previous government, it also existed under the previous one, and it will also exist under the next one, unless we now find some effective responses together. So, in this light, I would like to start the Question Time here with this invitation, which I hope the parties will accept constructively.

ParlaMint-DK_20160315145035	In the alternative, we would like to see the modern ceiling on cash aid removed completely from the table. We'd rather have cash assistance without counterclaims. Nevertheless, we have, of course, been involved in trying to exclude vulnerable groups from the ceiling on cash benefits and the 225-hour rule. This reform has a social battle page which has made it necessary to call seven consultations, a hearing, a question debate and three probably long readings in the Parliamentary Assembly and why so? Because it hits a very large group of the vulnerable, poor and sick people we have in Denmark. You deal with 150,000 people and put 30,000 people into cash aid. And why? To create 700 jobs. Fortunately, the centre-left parties have for the time being succeeded in excluding some of the most vulnerable groups with disabilities from the cash benefits ceiling and the 225-hour rule. The work of the two major opposition parties, the Social Democracy and the Enhedslisten, is to be commended for spending a great deal of resources on it. If we're gonna take it in big chunks, 75 percent. of the recipients of cash benefits, problems and challenges other than unemployment. This means that they have other problems, they need to be solved before they can take a job, and that again means that they are not directly susceptible to economic pressures or economic incentives, as it says. They just get poorer with the ceiling of cash aid, their children just get poorer, lots of people end up in poverty, impoverishment and misery without so we should exempt the 75%. cash benefits recipients who are not susceptible to economic pressure from the modern ceiling on cash benefits. Then we have the last 25%. of the recipients of cash benefits who can go directly to work, and of course they must also be exempted, because they want to work. In 2008, unemployment was down to 1.9%. This shows once and for all that people would like to work if there is work to be done within people's geographical and professional space. We do not need to force people to work, at least chasing people around for jobs that do not exist because we believe that unemployment is the fault of the unemployed. So let us continue this process until we have exempted all the people on whom the ceiling on cash benefits does not work, because then there is no one left in the target group, and then the ceiling does not matter. We want to help find the 700 jobs in another way. The Minister for Employment could have just asked for help in the Employment Committee to communicate 700 jobs from the start. In the alternative, we would like to help communicate jobs, and we would still rather be involved in creating new green and socially sustainable jobs. For us in the Alternative, the modern ceiling on cash aid has become a monument to the negative culture of our cash aid and employment system, where we abolish people's personal freedom and govern our fellow human beings with control and coercion. We have been confirmed by the fact that the employment area has been completely distorted from the outset. We are the country in the world where people have the most confidence in each other. We trust the police and the judiciary, the government and the public. We are world champions of trust. Yet we have a large area in our society where hundreds of thousands of people are governed by mistrust, coercion and control or so-called economic incentives. That is what we call unemployment. This has been the case for a long time, and many people almost think it has to be so. But we need to think new. Unemployment can affect all sections of the population, and it is probably common to all people, whether they vote for the DF, the Left or the Alternative, that they do not like to be deprived of responsibility for their own lives just because they have become unemployed. So as we fight side by side with the other centre-left parties against the social consequences of the cash aid ceiling, we are also working to build the future employment system based on trust, motivation and freedom. We will make it possible for people to retain responsibility for their own lives by allowing people to say no to offers that are not relevant to their way into society, offers that are not relevant to people's entry into the labour market. When we give that right back to people, we release billions of crowns that we use today to control each other, and some of them we need to use to make offers of job qualification, internship, personal development, courses, coaching so good that people accept, even if they are not forced to. To conclude, the alternative is against the entire modern ceiling on cash aid. We propose to exempt all target groups with which the ceiling makes no sense, that is. All of them. At the same time, we are building a system called cash assistance without counterclaims to restore the responsibility of the unemployed for their own lives. In this regard, we are holding a confidence parliament here at Christiansborg, where a large number of people across disciplines and beliefs are investigating how trust and not control and coercion can become a key resource in our cash assistance system. We must build an employment system in Denmark that is supported by trust.

ParlaMint-DK_20160315162112	Thank you. This bill is, of course, in some way a continuation of the previous discussion on the so-called cash benefits ceiling. I think that the word 'aid ceiling' is such a real spin word, because it makes it sound as if, before this so-called ceiling was introduced, there was only infinite cash aid, and that is not true. In other words, the ceiling on cash benefits means cutting benefits for people who already have very little to live on. What goes on in this bill with the spin words, because it's about expanding the circle of people who get so-called integration benefits. The word 'integration benefit' is at least as misleading as the word 'cash aid ceiling', because what is going to happen with this bill is that far more refugees in Denmark will live in poverty with massive cuts in their livelihoods. This does not create integration. On the contrary, you isolate people, and the isolation of people and make sure that they sit so hard in it that they may be able to pay the rent, but that they can at least not participate in any social life, is exceptionally stupid if you want good integration in Denmark. One argument for this bill could be to scare refugees away, so that refugees should say: No, we certainly do not want to come to Denmark; we do not have to go there. But that argument does not make sense, because this bill is not about those who are coming, but about those who already live here. Another argument could be that now I'm trying to understand the government's way of thinking about these refugees getting into work if you cut massively on their livelihoods. It has been asked what the employment impact of this bill is. In other words, how many people do you expect to get into work as a result of these drastic cuts, which are therefore subject to the imposition of refugees in Denmark? The employment effect appears to be so small that it cannot be measured. The government's answer is that this has not been measured. So you're willing to send thousands of people, including thousands of children, into poverty, knowing that the purpose of scaring refugees away has nothing to do with this bill, because the bill applies to people who already live here and knowing that the employment effect is so small that you don't even dare to measure it yourself. In other words, the consequence of this bill is one thing, and it's poverty. And if anything is bad for integration in Denmark, it is poverty. Why is that a problem? Well, that's a problem for the people it's hurting. The fact that you are not able to participate in social life and that you are at risk of social problems as a result of poverty is not funny. This is a problem for the people it affects. This is also a problem for everyone else in Danish society, because we do have a common interest in actually making integration work. Perhaps there is one place where this is going to create jobs, and that is among the social workers, because the municipal case officers now need to go through 200,000 cases to identify the 21,000 cases that are expected to be covered by this bill. In other words, those social workers who are already so insanely busy and who already have everything, too many cases on their tables with the consequences that can have for their work, now need to go through 200,000 cases manually to find out who it is you have to take the money from. A third point in connection with this bill that I would like to draw attention to is the obvious lack of cheap housing in Denmark. Now we heard earlier today, this morning, housing associations are out to say: Try to hear here, the mathematics that the government has put forward are completely unrealistic; so low rents are not there in the larger cities. We have heard several mayors say: we simply cannot see how to find a place where these people can live when they have so little to live for. ParlaMint-DK_20160315165042	I would like to thank the rapporteurs for their speeches and for their overwhelming support for the proposal. After all, the EU has adopted a regulation on eID and trust services, which will enter into force here on 1 July 2016. The regulation lays down a framework to ensure electronic transactions in the internal market, and in the long term this means that people and businesses will be able to use their electronic ID and signature across borders. The aim of the draft law is to repeal existing legislation and appoint a supervisory body and impose sanctions. In addition, the law must supplement the regulation with some provisions. The existing Danish regulation in the field • i.e. the law on electronic signatures is repealed with the entry into force of this bill, as the regulation regulates the same area as the law. The bill designates the Digitalisation Authority as the supervisory authority and provides for sanctions for breaching the regulation. Finally, the Minister of Finance is authorised to lay down rules on supervision and security in order to facilitate the work of the Business and Digitalisation Board on compliance with the requirements of the Regulation. I am, of course, ready to answer the various questions that may be raised at the end of the committee's deliberations.

In addition, wave energy, oil extraction, marine aquaculture are also activities that require space at sea, just as more traditional fishing and shipping do. Finally, there is also consideration for outdoor life and tourism. The intention to create clear guidelines and coordination for the use of the maritime space therefore welcomes the Social Democracy. We also welcome the fact that this work can also help to increase maritime growth opportunities, but nevertheless the new opportunities for growth at sea must not be at the expense of the environment. Therefore, we would also like to see the green profile in the bill tightened, so it is clear that the government is working to improve the Danish marine environment. The government should therefore be more inspired by Article 5 of the EU Directive, which specifically emphasises that one of the objectives of spatial maritime planning is the preservation, protection and improvement of the environment, including resilience to the consequences of climate change. We lack that balance in the purpose clause for the government's bill, where we believe that the balance tips and becomes too one-sidedly focused on growth. ParlaMint-DK_20160315170757	Thank you to the rapporteur. And the next rapporteur is Mr. Pelle Dragsted, Enhedslisten.
ParlaMint-DK_20160315170810	In the Enhedslisten, we agree entirely with the need for coherent planning of activities and installations at sea. And just like the law of plans for decades has been a good instrument for governing where in the country we would like to see development, for example. of our cities, where we would have liked to have seen nature in place, then planning at sea will also be useful to accommodate the different and opposing interests that, after all, compete over the sea area, and can be a good tool to also ensure our very vulnerable portrait. We think it is obvious that such a marine law will be based on the premises of portrait, since a healthy marine environment is the starting point for many other activities at sea to thrive and develop. This is particularly true of our fishing industry. That is why I was a little disappointed by reading down the draft law and was able to see that this is at least not sufficiently the case. The draft law does not have the marine environment as the central starting point for planning and, as we read it, it opens up the possibility that business interests may be over-influenced by considerations of the environment and nature. The draft law also chooses to give the Minister for Business and Growth the opportunity, without which we see it necessary to involve relevant ministers, to decide on actions that may have relatively significant consequences for the environment and nature. And we look at that with some concern. The draft law has chosen to emphasise the marine environment less strongly in the purpose of the law than is stated in the purpose of the directive implemented by this law. Thus, the title of the draft law, which is called the objective and scope, speaks only of sustainable growth, development and exploitation and not of the environmental aspects: ecosystem-based approach or conservation, protection and improvement of the environment. This may be seen later as something that must be taken into account in planning, but we see it as too much to make the marine environment secondary. We believe that at least it could have been ensured that the purpose of the law corresponds to the text of the directive and does not reduce it to some planning considerations. Because planning interests, including at sea, is a conflicting territory. We can, for example. take the Øresund, which is a rather unique area purely biologically. This is one of the few places where trawling has been prohibited since the 1930s, and it is one of the areas which has been allowed to develop very positively with a seabed flora and fauna and fish stocks which are considerably better than many other places in Danish waters. But here is, for example. have been granted permissions for dredging to supply Copenhagen's construction projects with sand. It's one of the kinds of conflict this law has to deal with, and it's a good example of the need to balance these things, and that nature considerations should be written in as something central to the marine plan. For of course, unique nature should not be rolled by some business interests. For example, if you leave it alone... In this case, dredging in the Øresund, the prospect of this extraction of raw materials is likely to overshadow nature in the future for many years to come, and therefore also important fishing grounds for the local fishing industry. We are destroying a natural basis that is essential for both tourism and leisure life, for something sand that might have been sucked up elsewhere at a marginal additional cost. We also think that there is a worrying authority for the Minister for Business and Growth in the bill, that is to say, the issue of how we read it, that it is the Minister for Business and Growth who has to establish the plan for the sea without involving other ministerial stakeholders. And that is a little disturbing from what I have just said. We also find it a little strange that the draft law allows the Minister for Business and Growth to lay down rules on when a decision can be appealed against and when it cannot be. It is a great power for the Minister, and it is also appealed to by many of the consultation parties in the public consultation that has been carried out. Unfortunately, however, this concern is completely rejected in the government's consultation note. So in the Enhedslisten, we are in favour of planning more, and we are in favour of a marine law, but we see some problems in this bill. We do not believe that there is enough emphasis on environmental considerations to be taken and we believe that business interests seem to take too much precedence over nature. That is why we will not support the draft law in its present form, but we hope, of course, that we have also heard other rapporteurs announce some amendments that during committee work we will be able to go somewhere where we will be able to see ourselves in the law.

ParlaMint-DK_20160315173813	There are no brief comments. Thank you to the rapporteur. The next rapporteur is Mr Seligman. Hans Andersen, Left. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20160315173819	Thank you. Today, we are dealing first with the proposal for a law on clinical trials of medicinal products. Innumerable experiments are currently being carried out on both humans and animals. These are trials to improve and refine treatment, the efficacy of medicinal products and reduce side effects, and this is beneficial for patients who have a need for treatment. It is absolutely crucial that, if we are to strengthen the development of new medicines and better treatment, which can benefit patients as soon as possible, we have to have a look to improve, adapt and make the framework for how clinical trials are not only approved but also followed up. Having said that, it is important for me to stress that it is essential for us in the Left that we always have a strong focus on the subjects, so that rights, security, dignity and well-being are protected as the bill says. Generally speaking, clinical trials are regulated in an EU regulation, which in Denmark is implemented in two legislations: the Committee Act and the Pharmaceutical Act. The draft law here brings together the pharmaceutical law and the committee law into a single law supplementing the regulation, and it will, in the future, be a new regulation of clinical trials on medicinal products on humans and animals. It is essential, when developing or further developing treatment methods, that this should be done in a safe and proper manner, so that both the rights of the subjects are respected and, at the same time, valid test data are obtained. The new regulation will ensure equal approval, implementation and monitoring of trials on medicinal products across the EU, as well as ensuring the rights of subjects, enhancing the quality of trials and increasing the number of trials on medicinal products with a better framework. In order to make the system in Denmark more flexible, it is proposed that the 12 regional committees dealing with pharmaceutical applications today should be brought together to fewer but more specialised committees specialising in drug trials. The Danish Medicines Agency and the Scientific Committees also have to coordinate applications and follow-up on experiments, and this means that new requirements for information exchange on e.g. the basis for validation of applications. One thing is the demands that must be made in the future to companies that apply for authorisation to carry out clinical trials and to the way in which supervision and authorisations are organised, something completely different is what to say about the people who participate in drug trials. It is vital that they be protected. Informed consent is a matter of course prior to participation in clinical trials, but there are groups which, for one reason or another, are unable to give informed consent, and those groups we have a special obligation to look after and protect. The regulation lays down stricter requirements for tests on persons who are unable to give their own consent, so that persons unable to act, minors and pregnant women meet with special protective measures. As I mentioned at the outset, the aim of the draft law here is that, among other things, there should be a ban on the use of hormones. an improvement in the framework for both patients and the pharmaceutical industry, but also the creation of a common EU portal for applications. This IT tool will ensure the exchange of data on clinical trials across the EU and will be expected to be completed in 2018. Finally, I should like to mention that the costs associated with the approval of clinical trials and their follow-up are financed by the stakeholders using the system. It can be private companies, hospitals and research institutions. In conclusion, the Left supports this proposal as it has been put forward. And then I should like to welcome the Conservative People's Party and say that they are also voting in favour of the bill.
ParlaMint-DK_20160315174249	There are no brief comments. Thank you to the rapporteur. The next rapporteur is Mrs Stine Brix, Enhedslisten. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20160315174257	Thank you. It is, of course, a relatively technically heavy bill to get through, I must admit, but when you get to the very last part, there is a rather controversial proposal among them. The rapporteur of the Danish People's Party has already mentioned this, and we have discussed it here in the Assembly earlier this is now 5-6 years ago and it concerns the possibility of making tests on patients who are unconscious. And that is controversial, because it is clear that when you are unconscious, you cannot consent to an experiment. Today it is the case that you can actually do tests on unconscious people, but you have to ask a doctor who is not involved in the experiment. He is then a trial guardian and has to give consent, and then he has to give his own consent. What lies in this proposal is the removal of the requirement that there should be an experimental guardian. We cannot support this on the part of the Enhedslisten. We already find it highly controversial that tests can be carried out on patients who cannot give their consent because they are unconscious and that additional requirements can be removed so that they can be done more easily, we are not in favour of them. I also think that there are some other elements in the draft law that deserve some comments. First of all, I believe that the rapporteur of the Left has just called it flexibility. Yeah, you could call it that. One might also call it what I think it is: a violent centralisation of the system we have which takes a position on ethics in experiments. It is the case today that we have scientific ethics committees, where there are both ordinary representatives of citizens and patients, what we call lay members, and then there are health professionals. They sit regionally, and what we're going to see with this bill is that many decisions are moved centrally to the central scientific ethics committees. Here there will be less representation of ordinary citizens than there is today, and there will be the possibility that the President, who is to be a health professional, will be able to make decisions without consulting the Scientific Committee if the deadlines so require. And it's actually the same thing in this bill that the deadlines are pretty tight. I would say that it is rare for me to see legislative proposals that regulate deadlines as detailed as this one. We're down to 5 days to one, 7 days to the other, 12 days to the third and 26 days in total, etc. Imagine if citizens had the same rights around, for example. in relation to having a complaint dealt with in the Appeals Board for the Patient Compensation. It was a parenthesis. But that means that this power, which the President has, can take decisions if the deadline so requires, can actually be timely and we risk taking decisions about what scientific experiments we will approve in Denmark without there being a real debate about it. All in all, I think it is a legislative proposal and a regulation from the European Union that takes far too much account of the pharmaceutical industry, research institutions and others who want to research, and to far too little account of the citizen's perspective. The balance has simply been tipped in the wrong direction. Perhaps this is because what you want with this is, what to say, to make it easier to make scientific experiments, but also from a Danish perspective to make yourself attractive in the competition to attract experiments. I think that's what helps you get so eager to look attractive. So the bottom line is that we are voting no, just as we did back 5-6 years ago, the last time this matter was dealt with.
ParlaMint-DK_20160315174711	Thank you to the rapporteur. There are no brief comments. The next rapporteur is Mrs May-Britt Kattrup, Liberal Alliance. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20160315174718	Thank you. Experiments and research are crucial to the development of medicines and treatments, which are of great importance for our survival and our quality of life. However, it is important that the conditions surrounding the trials respect the rights and safety of subjects. The main purpose of the regulation is to safeguard the rights of subjects and therefore the Liberal Alliance supports the proposal. A further aim is to improve the quality of experiments and increase the number of experiments. New procedures must provide shorter time limits and a common EU portal for applications is established. The starting point is that subjects must always give prior consent prior to participation in research projects, and this is a very important point for the Liberal Alliance. In the case of minors, consent shall be given by the holder of custody. However, if the person has reached the age of 15, he or she must also give his or her consent and it must be respected if he or she does not wish to participate. The Regulation also provides that, as far as possible, subjects between 5 and 15 years of age should be consulted on their position on participation in the trial and, if it is considered that they are able to have a position, that position should be respected. For persons under guardianship, consent must be given by the guardian. If, under guardianship, the person is able to form an opinion, an explicit request from the person concerned shall be taken into account. In the case of persons unable to act, the consent of the next of kin and of a so-called trial guardian who is a doctor who is responsible for the subject's interests but who must be independent of the trial, both financially and professionally. In acute cases such as heart attack or stroke, acute drug trials may be carried out without prior consent if a number of requirements are met, including: that it is not possible to obtain prior consent; that the trial may confer a direct clinical benefit on the subject; that the trial carries a minimal risk and burden on the standard treatment; and that the subject has not previously raised any objections to participating in the clinical trial. I would like to ask you to take up the provisions relating to urgent cases in the further work of the committee. I hope that the Minister will be open to a more precise wording. I do not find it so reassuring that it says that the experiment can but, as I said, the Liberal Alliance supports the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20160315175437	Thank you to the rapporteur. There are no brief comments. The next speaker is the Minister for Health and the Elderly. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20160315175451	Thank you. First of all, I would like to thank all the rapporteurs for a good and nuanced debate today, which I fully recognise is based on a legislative proposal which is also complicated on a number of points. However, I think that today's debate shows the entire Parliament's interest in finding together the best possible solutions for clinical trials on medicinal products. This is important, and it is for many reasons. It is, first and foremost, because those experiments are a key element in the process of developing new and better medicines. Tests shall help to demonstrate whether a medicinal product is working and whether there is the necessary safety and quality. And only when the scientific evidence is in order must a medicine be on the market. A side effect of this is that clinical trials help promote growth and ensure good jobs in the pharmaceutical industry, including here in Denmark. We should therefore also be pleased that there will be a new regulation with a comprehensive and common regulation of trials across the European Union. The rules of the bill must ensure that this regulation can work in Denmark. Both the regulation and the legislative proposal are based on key principles of the current regulation. This applies in particular to the continuation of a number of quality standards on good clinical practice, but it also applies to the general principle that trials may only be conducted if the rights and safety of the subjects are protected. Consent to participation is absolutely crucial in this context. As we have discussed in the debate here today, it may be the consent of the subject himself or a specially appointed representative. Here we must, among other things. Consider that a trial may not only be a risk to the subjects; it may also be a good chance to try a more effective treatment. The regulation will also make a number of significant changes. In particular, they shall be introduced: greater technical cooperation between countries and greater transparency of experimental data. Of particular importance is the introduction of a new model for the assessment of experimental applications. In future, they must be submitted to a common EU portal, where they are dealt with in procedures with short deadlines between the countries where an experiment is intended to be carried out. However, the ethical aspects of the subjects still need to be assessed separately in each country. In order for the new application procedure to work in practice, it is necessary to adjust the procedures between the Danish Medicines Agency and the committee system. I am very confident that the proposed organisation of the committee system will ensure that it is operational. The setting-up of independent committees specializing in drug trials must necessarily promote a qualified procedure which can be specifically targeted on specific aspects of medicinal products. At the same time, I expect that the centralisation of the assignment will facilitate cooperation between the pharmaceutical committees and the Danish Medicines Agency, both in relation to the assessment of applications within strict time limits and in the ongoing monitoring and control of individual trials. The requirements for consent, including in acute situations, are largely contained in the Regulation. At the same time, however, I am also satisfied that the requirement for a substitute consent is left to national legislation. This allows us to draw up rules that are precisely suited to Danish views in the sensitive situations where a subject cannot give his or her own consent. It is my impression that there is satisfaction with the proposed requirements for the substitution consent of the different groups of subjects. Especially in the hearing, there has been such a positive attitude to the new concept of guardianship that it is wanted to be introduced as soon as possible. I have, of course, agreed to this with a proposed entry into force on 1 July of this year. I have also responded to a number of other requests from the hearing, among others. a statement that the access to medical records proposed by the Committee Act and the pharmaceutical law will also enter into force on 1 July of this year. These are merely clarifications and adjustments in relation to the current rules on access to data as part of checks. It is only good to have those rules clarified as soon as possible. We could then continue to review the new regulation that we will have with the regulation and the legislative proposal for a long time, but it will take some time before the regulation is finally ready for use. First, some major IT systems for the exchange of data between countries need to be developed at both EU and national level. They are expected to be ready by 2018. And when the regulation is ready, the new law on clinical trials must come into force at the same time. But even if the law does not come into force for a few years, I see it as a great advantage that the proposal will be adopted in this session. This allows us to start now implementing the proposed reorganisation of the whole committee system. Then the committees are quite ready when the overall regulation is also in place, and until then, fortunately, we have both the committee law and the pharmaceutical law. I look forward to the further debate on the bill. I was able to hear that there was already an alert on a number of follow-up questions as part of the further examination of the committee which, of course, I am making available to you in order to get an answer. Moreover, I look forward to continued good cooperation on the legislative proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20160315180629	Thank you very much, President. This bill is about a change in the health code, and it is the right to a rapid investigation and a guarantee of treatment. For us Social Democrats, it is crucial that all Danes can count on free and equal access to fast investigation and treatment of high quality if they become ill. It must not be the thickness of the wallet or your postal code that determines which treatment you can receive. We will never accept a division into an A and a B team, and we will always fight inequality. We will always fight for the Danes to have as much health as possible for the money. That is why we are opposed to over-treatment and over-payment of private hospitals. The proposal we are considering today contains three parts. Firstly, the study patients, i.e. patients who have to have a diagnosis of a possible disease, will be included in the extended free hospital choice when, for capacity reasons, the region cannot provide them within one month. Secondly, patients who have been referred and assessed will have the right to seek treatment elsewhere if the region cannot offer them treatment within one month. Thirdly, that the Minister should also be able to lay down requirements for the content and design of the information materials that patients must already have on their rights regarding the extended free choice of hospital. When we sat with government responsibility, one of our main priorities was to reduce waiting times, and we succeeded. The waiting time was at the lowest level ever when we left government offices. We achieved this result with a combination of a strong political focus, a good job from both health workers and the regions, and a sensible involvement of both public and private capacities. In fact, the number of referrals from the regions to private actors struck all records in the first half of 2015, and it must be noted that without the cost taking the same flight. We are proud of this in the Social Democratic Party. But short wait for treatment is not worth much if you cannot quickly find out what is wrong. That is why we introduced a one-month right of investigation for both physical and mental disorders. Exploration and treatment go hand in hand, but we must also recognise that, although a reinforced focus on investigation is the right way to go, the right way to do this has not worked well enough. And that is why we are in favour of patients being given the opportunity to look elsewhere if the region cannot complete them within the time limit. However, it is also important for us that this new space of opportunity for the patient does not end up as a vacuum where the patient is left alone, as several consultation answers point out. As far as the treatment guarantee is concerned, we share the intention that patients should be treated quickly. The waiting times, as I said earlier, were a central focus of the previous government. It must remain so, and therefore it is important that all available capacity, public and private, be involved for the benefit of patients. As I explained earlier, there was also a broad involvement of private actors within our government, and we note with satisfaction that the government has now chosen, in its consultation note, to listen more to the Competition Authority than to the private hospitals when it comes to the regions' ability to negotiate reasonable prices in the future too. Against this background, we see no immediate danger that the golden cocks will run again, as they did in the '00s, and that is why we can also support this part of the proposal. However, we intend, in close cooperation with patients' associations, professional organisations and regions, to keep a close eye on the consequences of the proposal, because it is important that the proposal does not lead to over-payment, over-treatment or flight of personnel from the public sector such as the one we saw in the 00s. In addition, the proposal must not hinder coherent patient processes or lead to a downgrading of the patient groups referred to in the analysis and treatment in the primary sector, such as private practitioners. That is why we will also be working to ensure that, after a few years, we have a thorough evaluation of the effects and consequences of this bill. The Socialists support the proposal with those comments.

ParlaMint-DK_20160315183647	Thank you. Today we are first treating the law on amending the Health Act, which is about a real right to rapid investigation and free hospital choice at 30 days' waiting time for treatment. The health of the Danes has always been a priority of the Left, and it will also be forward-looking. In the 2016 Finance Act, we have prioritised health and the elderly by 3.4 billion. kr., and that's a significant boost. In the government base it is clearly stated that strengthening the rights of Danish patients is a high priority. All patients must have a real right to rapid investigation, as it says. An old Left slogan says, "Man before the system." Although it is an old slogan, it is nevertheless what the draft law is all about today. With the bill here, as it stands, we strengthen the fundamental rights of Danish patients. In concrete terms, this means that Danish patients can now look forward to a real right to rapid investigation within one month. It is therefore a right of the patient to extended hospital choice when, for reasons of capacity, the region cannot offer an explanation within one month. That's common sense. For us, it is not crucial whether you are being investigated or treated in a public or private hospital. The Left's clear position is that all Danes should have the right to prompt treatment and investigation, whether they are physically or mentally ill. Unfortunately, the previous government, the SR government, reduced the VK government's 30-day treatment guarantee, and instead, the Danes received a so-called differentiated guarantee, which in practice meant that some patients could be treated within the framework of free hospital choice within 30 days, while the right to free hospital choice for other patients only enters into force after a waiting period of more than 60 days. The differentiated guarantee is basically an expression of bureaucratic thinking which puts the system before man and not vice versa. The VK Government's treatment guarantee, on the other hand, proved its worth. Last left was in government, the average waiting time for all operated dropped from 90 days in 2001 to 52 days in September 2011. But if we really have to ensure that we put the human being before the system, then it is also essential that you as a patient know your rights and get the right information. The former government's right of investigation is a hollow right because it is not really guaranteed the possibility of going to where the waiting time is shortest and thus opting out of the queue. All too often we have heard that the level of information can vary from region to region, which is why I am delighted that both Danske Regioner, the Association for Private Hospitals and Clinics and Dansk Occupation are positive in ensuring that correct information material is prepared for patients, so that as a patient you can make a hospital choice on an informed basis. As mentioned at the outset, the treatment guarantee and the right of investigation must be seen as part of a major overall health boost. Although the enhanced patient rights must be clearly seen in the light of this overall boost, they are also in themselves an expression of a fundamental attitude to how we weigh the relationship between system and individual. Where the previous government weakened patients' rights and thus the individual's opportunities in the system, we are now strengthening patients' rights and the individual's opportunities in the meeting with the health service, because all Danish patients now have real rights. Against this background, the Left will support this bill. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20160315185551	Thank you. The proposal here strengthens patients' rights so that they have an opportunity to opt out of waiting if it exceeds 30 days. For more than 30 days of waiting for investigation or treatment, patients will have the right to choose one of the hospitals, clinics or other services with which the Regional Councils have agreed or cooperate. In this way, a right to choose a private alternative is created when the public authorities cannot offer clarification or treatment within 30 days. This means shorter waiting times not only for those who can afford to pay a private alternative for themselves, which means that we will have less inequality in this proposal. For the Liberal Alliance, it is very positive that patients come closer to a free choice than they are today, but free choice only works if there is something to choose from and you know about the options. It is therefore important for us that it is not unnecessarily complicated when private hospitals, clinics, etc. shall be approved to conclude an agreement, but shall be subject to equal conditions. It is also very important for patients to be aware of their rights and opportunities. Information about the right to choose as well as the places to choose and, above all, information about the different places is important. The information may be about geographical location, waiting times and professional expertise or other important factors. The information shall be easily accessible and easily understandable. Many patients themselves do not have a surplus to acquire knowledge of rights and opportunities, and it must therefore be the responsibility of doctors, hospitals and regions to inform patients of their choices. A free choice is only worth something if there is something to choose from and you know what you can choose. It is important that a course be as coherent as possible. Therefore, as far as possible, it is necessary to avoid dividing up the process between different actors, but in some cases, splitting up the course can lead to faster treatment, and patients will have that possibility with this agreement. The Liberal Alliance would very much like the regions to draw up a capacity inventory and to establish a realistic plan for their own ability to offer the services requested within a given period of time. We would like to see the use of private hospitals and clinics proactively planned instead of using them only as emergency solutions and waiting until the last moment to involve private people. Such planning could contribute to shorter waiting times, better patient outcomes and lower prices and better use of capacities, as well as better opportunities for the private to provide the requested service. I would like to see an even more freer choice, but this proposal is certainly an improvement for patients, and LA therefore supports the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20160315190457	Thank you very much. The aim of the bill is to give all patients the right to rapid investigation and a following right to extended free hospital choice if, for capacity reasons, the region cannot offer an investigation within one month and at more than one month's waiting time for treatment. We welcome the speedier way out, but we are very critical of the extended free choice of hospital if the waiting time for treatment is above the strict limit of 1 month. The proposal therefore removes the differentiated right to treatment which in practice allows public hospitals to make a professionally based priority of tasks. It also puts pressure on the public hospital services in some areas where they are not able to scale up, among other things. Because of a lack of specialist doctors. We would also like the investigation and treatment processes not to be so badly divided as is the case with the proposal here, but that it might be better integrated when necessary. But the very big crunch for us is therefore the removal of the differentiated right to treatment, which deprives hospitals of the opportunity to prioritise wisely with their own resources, naturally taking account of professionalism. The alternative cannot therefore support the proposal, and I should like to welcome the rapporteur of the Radicals and say that they do not support the proposal either.
ParlaMint-DK_20160315190613	There are no brief comments, and we would like to thank Mr Seligman. Nikolai Amstrup. The next rapporteur is Mr Seligman. Jonas Dahl from SF.
ParlaMint-DK_20160315190625	Thank you. I would like to start by saying that we in the SF cannot support the bill. I think that a number of improvements were made under the previous government, and I would like to acknowledge that the Socialists agreed with us at the time, which is why I am rather saddened by what we have heard from the Socialists today. This has, of course, been quite a turnaround, especially in relation to the treatment guarantee. But leave it at first. I think that, having read the bill and also heard the rapporteurs from the Left and a number of the bourgeois parties, we have to say that there are a number of things on which we may perhaps be able to agree, namely that we must constantly look at how we can do things better, and it is also difficult to disagree. However, although we agree on the diagnosis, I do not think we agree on what kind of treatment is needed. Because, of course, we have to do something about the waiting times that are still too long, both for investigation and for treatment in hospitals. I also agree that, in addition to this, we may have to do something more serious than that, but I think that where we go completely wrong, we are in relation to the means. I think it is unacceptable that it should be private hospitals that should solve capacity problems in the public sector. It does not surprise me that it is a Left Government which proposes that you should now be curling parents for private hospitals. We have seen this before, but I am surprised that others have also jumped on the idea. And I think there are several reasons why this is a bad idea. Firstly, we run the risk of undermining the coherence of our common healthcare system, and it is simply no use that the money we pay to create a functioning system should go to private actors whose main driving force is ultimately to generate profits. We looked up the results through the '00s, and we saw how the Reichsaudit also contested it and concluded that it would have cost the Treasury a quite significant million dollars if not a billion dollars. I think that speaks for itself, and it also means that we need to have some reservations about how we use private actors. Secondly, we are also running the risk that more and more of the expensively trained doctors we want more and need in public health care, not least in the outermost regions, are now seeking higher salaries in the private sector. In this way, society will once again have to pay extra to make use of the investment we have made in training them. Once again, we take money from the public sector and reduce public services, and as Danske Regioner also points out in the consultation answers, there will be no more specialist specialists who will be given more and more jobs in private hospitals, let alone allow them to be assigned to private hospitals, because this must ultimately be the development we are going to see. On the contrary, it will mean that those who have more than wool in their pockets will come first and easiest to the treatments. Yes, I do not really understand how this is going to strengthen patients' rights, because those who end up being losers in this game are those who cannot afford the health insurance that we will probably soon see further strengthening. Thirdly, we are also familiar with the history of private hospitals. We saw it under the present Prime Minister, who himself sat as health minister in the golden era of private hospitals. I think the then Deputy Prime Minister called it the Golden Cocks. And it must also be said that the Prime Minister knows very well what happened, both with overpayment to private hospitals, but not least with the extended free choice of hospital. I also believe that this is in line with the consultation responses from the Medical Association and the FOA, which also points out that it is a bad idea to remove the differentiation in the treatment guarantee introduced by the previous government. It is, after all, a question of ensuring that those with the most serious diseases are treated as soon as possible, but it also means that we have to give priority, even at a time of narrow and limited resources, so that the most serious diseases are dealt with first, and that everything else just means that those with less serious diseases can wait longer. I also believe that, as politicians, we need to do this with open eyes, rather than sticking people's eyes in the eye and saying that everyone can be treated equally quickly. This cannot be done, and the answers to the hearings also clearly testify to this. There are also some problems associated with the division between analysis and treatment. Where the division of responsibilities is to lie is not entirely clear, and I expect the Minister to reply to that too, perhaps not later today, but then in the next reading of the committee. I can be concerned about what responsibility it is for, how the investigation will ultimately take place and how it will ensure that patients can then take advantage of the opportunity they say that patients will now be given. Of course, this does not change the fact that patients have a right to better rights, a right to rapid investigation and a right to good treatment, and how do we ensure that this is done? All in all, there are some things in this proposal that are yet another attempt to empty the public purses and throw the money at the private. And that is another proposal on the long slippery slope, I would call it that which contributes to greater inequality in society, and that is why the solution is not to continue along that path either, but to go the other way, namely to strengthen our public health services and to invest much more in prevention than is proposed by the current government, and it is very much about strengthening public health services, which will then also be able to solve the capacity challenges that we have to recognise that there are. That is why this is about investing, but it is also about investing wisely and, above all, doing so in the common public health care system.

I know from the Capital Region that the birth preparation for a number of women has been discontinued because there was a lot of illness. You can have that in the neighboring region. Even within a region like the capital there are great differences. In some places they have a lot of work in the maternity ward and find it difficult to reach it. Then you can come to Hillerød and feed in the sensory feeding room, where there is very, very good time and very good conditions. So that way, there are differences in our healthcare system. ParlaMint-DK_20160315192000	Thank you very much to Mrs Mette Abildgaard. There are no brief comments. The last in the line is the Minister for Health and the elderly.
ParlaMint-DK_20160315192017	Thank you. Thank you to the rapporteurs for receiving the bill and for the debate here today. We have come a long way, and I am glad that waiting times and patient rights take us all into account, albeit with a somewhat different starting point. Thank you, however, for the statements that have been made in the course of the debate. As a minister, I note with great satisfaction that, in general terms, there is support for the purpose of the draft law by all the consultation parties, not least patients supporting it. I think that is important, because it is they that are ultimately involved. It was also with pleasure, but also with a rather great surprise, that this morning I had to see how broad a support there is in the Danish Parliament for the government's proposal. There are many things that have changed, said the Socialist rapporteur, and indeed that is something that has to be said. A lot of things have changed. I am thinking, of course, of the huge turnaround we have witnessed today from the Social Democrats, including in this area. I am, of course, pleased, on behalf of patients, that the Social Democratic Party is now also on the side of patients. I'll get back to that. But first a little bit about the bill we are discussing today. We want a strong public health service. That is why the government, together with the blue parties with which we have entered into financial legislation, is raising the health and elderly area by 3.4 billion this year. kr. extra. Of course, when you get sick, you need to know quickly what's wrong with you. A quick analysis is the prerequisite for getting treatment as a sick person in time and quickly returning to everyday life with family and with work. That is why, on the part of the government, we will allow all patients to choose shorter waiting times for investigation and treatment. In short, we want to strengthen patients' rights. This is true regardless of whether it is a physical or a mental illness, and no matter where you live in the country. We want an extension of free choice for investigation and we want an extension of free choice for treatment at a waiting time of 1 month and not of 2 months. The reason is, of course, that we have unfortunately seen that the regions have failed to fulfil their obligations to provide treatment for patients referred to within 30 days where it is technically possible. In fact, only about six out of ten patients are released within the deadline. So far too many people are still waiting far too long to be solved, and there are very large differences in waiting times across the country, across the regions. According to the current rules, the right of investigation, as it was adopted under the previous government, today the patients' only specific right remains in the right to appeal to the Patient Safety Agency. It is not a right that is good enough for the patient. The patient must also have the right to opt out of long waiting times if there is a possibility of receiving rapid treatment elsewhere. It should not be the case that if you have the resources to do so, you should be able to go out and buy yourself for quick investigation and treatment. We don't think that kind of inequality should be there. It must be possible for everyone to be rescued and treated quickly without undue waiting. It has to be regardless of purse and resources. You shouldn't be able to buy yourself or talk yourself out in the queue. Then, of course, I am well aware that there may be perfectly legitimate reasons why not all patients can be cared for within 30 days. It can, for example, be done. be because it is not technically possible, or because a patient has chosen for himself that one wants to be ridden out in a particular hospital and therefore actively chooses to wait longer. On the other hand, I think that there is every reason to help patients who continue to wait not because they have chosen it for themselves, but because of capacity reasons, and to give them the right to opt out of waiting if there is available capacity elsewhere. That is what the government wants with that bill. That was the right of investigation, then there's the right of treatment. It was originally introduced by the former VK government back in 2002. It helped to ensure that we had more than five weeks of waiting shaved at that time. The former SR government then got on a good day reduced the waiting times by 5 days, so you can make the comparison yourself and see what has been achieved by success. Then the Social Democrat rapporteur said in his speech that they will never accept an A and B team, but that is exactly what they did then. That's when the patients were divided into the A-team and the B-team. The A-team were those who, with the purse in order or the insurance policy in hand, could step out of the long queues of public health care and get the short wait and get more quickly into treatment. That is what we brought up when we introduced the guarantee of treatment, the right of treatment, the extended freedom of hospital choice. It was a right which the previous government then chose to undermine by introducing the so-called differentiated right to extended freedom of hospital choice; it must therefore now be understood that at least parts of the previous government have come to a better mind. More people came to wait longer, and they were not the ones who could pay for it themselves. Nor were they the ones who could make their case to the doctor so that the doctor could understand that there was some urgency. The government does not want that, and I now understand that it no longer wants social democracy. As I said, I really appreciate it on behalf of patients. Therefore, the government will also abolish the differentiated treatment right and replace it with a real right to extended free hospital choice for all patients, whether they suffer from a physical or a mental illness, if there is more than one month's waiting time for treatment. The goal is not for more patients to opt out of the public sector. The aim is that more patients are treated more quickly and that, within the framework of a strong public health service, we ensure the best treatment as soon as possible and that we get as much health as possible for the money. I think we owe it to patients to have equal access to the option of opting out if the regions are unable to offer treatment within one month. Then there is another important point about the bill: we are now strengthening patient information. I would like to thank the Conservative rapporteur for his good comments on this. I have heard from a great many citizens in the past, and it is also clear from the evaluation of the law of December 2015 that there is a need for better and more uniform information for patients about their rights. It has also been expressed by various quarters in the hearings. As a patient, we must, of course, be clearly informed about what rights we have and, therefore, the draft law also provides for a new legal basis for the Minister of Health to be able to lay down requirements for the information given to patients. The regions, representatives of patients and private hospitals will be involved in this work, and the aim is that patients receive the same information about their rights, regardless of which public hospital they are referred to, and that the information is presented in an easy and understandable way to patients. I am very pleased that there is such broad support for the individual parts of the proposal. All that we are dealing with in the draft law today is the same thing that I proposed and tabled amendments to when we dealt with the deterioration of the treatment guarantee by the previous government and a so-called investigation right, which was therefore only the right to appeal to the Patient Safety Agency. There we tabled amendments to introduce a real right of rapid investigation with an extended freedom of hospital choice and to maintain a reinforced guarantee of treatment with the right to rapid treatment within 30 days. It was voted down by the Social Democratic Party. There they were in government. Now there has been opposition, and today it is announced that we will vote in favour of this proposal, which was then voted down last time. I noted that as recently as December 2015, the Socialist rapporteur spoke to the Jyllands-Posten, three months ago, about the bill from the government, and I quote: Here one clearly senses that we have a Left Government. The new proposal is of no benefit to patients. Quote out. That was three months ago, but today we have to understand that the world is different, at least from the Social Democrats, which are running away from everything they have said in the past. I think it is good not for the confidence in the political promise, but for the patients that we are now more in the Parliament hall to support strengthening their rights, whether they have a physical or mental illness. So thank you for that turnaround and hence for the broad support in the Parliamentary Chamber for this bill, a support that I did not expect would be so wide. But a new day, new opportunities, so thank you for the broad support. I look forward, of course, to further discussions and make myself available to answer any questions that may arise in connection with the further examination of the committee.

ParlaMint-DK_20160315200331	Thank you. Where are the inexpensive housing that cash benefit recipients have to move into when they are put out of their apartment because they cannot pay the rent because of the ceiling on cash benefits? How is this proposal to avoid leading to increased poverty? That is how it happened in the '00s when we had a version of the ceiling on cash benefits and the hourly rule, which was not nearly as hard as the one that is being introduced now. ParlaMint-DK_20160315200616	Thank you. Then it is the Minister for Employment to answer.
ParlaMint-DK_20160315200628	Thank you. It is the Government's ambition that we should have more in employment. That is why, with the proposal on the ceiling for cash benefits and the 225-hour rule, we will increase the gain by getting into employment. For many recipients of cash benefits today, there is no noticeable difference between being on cash benefits and being in work. At the same time, there is today a group of cash aid recipients who are actually not available for the labour market. The most recent figures show that this is about one in four persons employed in the field of cash benefits. That is also why we are now introducing a ceiling on the total benefits that can be received in the cash benefits system. At the same time, we are calling for cash benefits recipients who can work to meet a working requirement of 225 hours within a total of one year. The 225 hours correspond to this approx. 5-6 hours of work per week or 11⁄2 months of full-time employment. The ceiling on cash benefits and the 225-hour rule must ensure that more cash benefit recipients become part of the working community. At the same time, the ceiling on total benefits should be seen in the context of the second phase of job reform, where the government wants to ease the tax burden on people in employment. This will further increase the benefit of, and hence the incentive to work. The draft law represents a fair balance between incentives to work and a reasonable temporary substitute. It is clear, however, that this means, of course, that as a cash aid recipient you must give priority to your expenditure, as is the case for citizens in employment. It is also my clear view and that of the government that if we are to break the social legacy and support the education and entry of the next generation into the labour market, the best thing we can do for those families is to get parents into work. It has been crucial for the government and the parties to the agreement that the bill creates the right balance between a reasonable level of remuneration for all on the one hand and a real incentive to work on the other. The ceiling on cash benefits applies as a starting point for all persons receiving integration benefit, education or cash benefits. However, for most of the recipients of cash benefits affected by the ceiling on cash benefits, the total reduction in benefits will be less than DKr 2 000 and the average reduction in benefits will be approx. 1,450 kr. per month. Specifically, the ceiling on cash benefits means that a typical couple with three children will continue to have approx. 15,000 kroner. available per month when tax and rent are paid, or approx. 406,000 kr. in total pre-tax public benefits, when starting from family type calculations. In my view, this is a reasonable level of dependency, while at the same time the rules allow individuals to avoid, in whole or in part, a reduction in special aid or housing aid by working a few hours a week. At the same time, bearing in mind that cash benefits are temporary benefits and that people who cannot work at all should not be in the cash benefits system, we must, of course, have levels of benefits that support the fact that working is worthwhile. In the draft law and the amendment, we have excluded various groups of people with disabilities from the ceiling on cash benefits. This applies, firstly, to persons who are severely handicapped and who are searched for a suitable accommodation by the municipality. The housing aid will not be included in the ceiling here. This applies regardless of whether it is the recipient of cash benefits himself or a member of the household who is not a recipient of cash benefits who is severely handicapped. Then we have the amendment, among other things. on the basis of the KL's hearing answer, chosen to exempt special support and housing support from the ceiling for cash benefits recipients who, due to a physical or mental impairment, have been searched for and have been assigned a suitable accommodation by the municipality. In addition, both special support and housing support are exempted from the ceiling for persons with reduced mobility and persons receiving 24-hour care under the Services Act who have found a suitable accommodation themselves, i.e.: in situations where the dwelling is not designated by the municipality. Persons with such limited working capacity that, on the basis of a concrete individual assessment, the municipality considers that the persons concerned will not be able to obtain 225 hours of employment within one year in the ordinary labour market, are exempted from the increased availability requirement. The vast majority of people who have been assessed as ready for activity, e.g. Therefore, due to disability, abuse, mental illness, etc., is expected to be exempted from the 225-hour requirement. At the same time, it should be stressed that the amended rules do not require the citizen to immediately enter full-time or part-time employment with a high number of hours. Only 225 hours of work are required within one year, which means that with 5-6 hours of work a week you will be able to meet the requirement. This means that short-term small jobs are also an option. Of course, economic incentives must not be left alone and we must do everything we can to support citizens in getting into jobs at the same time. For a job is the best way to a safe and stable life for the individual family. I do not think that we should abandon the ambition of a job and the increased quality of life that it carries with it for the group of cash aid recipients who are not currently ready for work. It is precisely the job centres' job to help citizens get closer to the labour market or participate in education. The municipality has a duty to help and support the unemployed to enter the labour market through an individual contact process and an active effort. The individual contact process means that there must be regular conversations between the job centre and the unemployed person. The talks shall ensure that the actions undertaken are followed up. Job search etc. must be discussed and the next step towards employment must be agreed. Active action must be organised on the basis of the individual's resources and needs and may, for example, be: This appropriation is intended to cover expenditure on studies, meetings of experts, information and publications directly linked to the achievement of the objectives of the programme, and any other expenditure on technical and administrative assistance not involving public authority tasks outsourced by the Commission under ad hoc Existing knowledge indicates that enterprise-oriented efforts have the best employment impact, including for vulnerable target groups. That was among other things. a central conclusion of the committee work carried out by Carsten Koch for the former government. Therefore, we would like to support the work of the municipalities to get vulnerable cash aid recipients going on the business. At the end of 2015, therefore, an experiment was launched under the name of JobFirst, where vulnerable citizens have to go to real jobs from day one and where the goal is ordinary employment for as many hours as possible. In addition, with the 2016 rate pool, 78,2 million has been allocated. kr. to specially organised business programmes to encourage job centres to get the vulnerable unemployed in a business-oriented effort at ordinary jobs that can prepare them for an ordinary job. Activity-specific recipients of training or cash assistance are also entitled to a coordinating case officer to ensure that action is coordinated across municipal administrations. There is therefore a focus on helping people to achieve permanent employment as soon as possible and full or partial self-employment or ordinary education. As also stated in the comments to the bill, the municipalities should be specifically paying extra attention to providing guidance and assistance to persons receiving cash benefits as they approach the reduction in benefits as a result of the 225-hour rule. The municipalities can, for example, in connection with the contact process. offer these citizens a business consultant with special industry knowledge and knowledge of job opportunities in the local labour market. Citizens with significant and permanently reduced working capacity are not included in the cash benefits system. It is therefore also important for me to make it clear once again that citizens who are more permanently unable to work at all, for example. because of illness, should not be in the cash benefits system at all. The municipalities have a duty here to ensure that the citizens receive the right help. In addition, municipalities must systematically follow up rehabilitation teams on whether a citizen, for example: must be searched for a resource or flex job or have granted early retirement if the person otherwise fulfils the conditions for it. The parties to the agreement have paid particular attention to this, and it is therefore also clear from the draft law that the Ministry of Employment will launch an evaluation in 2017 to highlight the extent to which vulnerable cash benefits recipients will have their working capacity and dependency clarified. In fact, in relation to the opportunities for getting a job, I believe that we have a well-functioning and dynamic labour market in which many jobs are continuously created. Currently, a number of employers even mention that they find it difficult to fill vacancies. On jobnet.dk there were vacancies in January of the year 27,577. Of these, 18.123 were newly created posts. At the same time, job vacancies on Jobnet represent only part of the job vacancies in January. Very many vacancies in Denmark are filled, for example. via networks and informal channels. During the period from the fourth quarter of 2014 to the third quarter of 2015, there were almost 776,000 job openings in Denmark. This underlines that there is a job to be obtained. This should also be seen in the light of the fact that in October 2015, almost 155,000 full-time foreign nationals worked in Denmark. I can tell you that a new ceiling on cash benefits, a 225-hour rule, etc. According to the government's long-term amendments overall, it is estimated that public finances will increase by some 538.5 million. kr. after tax and backflow and including behavioral consequences. At the same time, the proposal is expected to increase employment by about 700 years' work or full-time workers. I believe that, with the ceiling on cash benefits and the 225-hour rule, we are taking the cash benefits system in the right direction. It is absolutely essential that there is always an incentive to work and that we ensure that the recipients of cash benefits are actually available for the labour market. The changes we are making represent a fair balance, in which everyone is still guaranteed a reasonable remuneration. In addition, there are still a number of options for providing additional help to the individual after an individual and concrete assessment. Both the rules on the ceiling and the 225-hour rule provide for the necessary exceptions, so that we ensure that, for example, disabled people who have the necessary higher cost of housing are not affected inappropriately and those who are currently unable to work do not lose their help because of the requirement for 225 hours of work. Thank you.

But it's not like that. Right must be right. In fact, there is a single organisation that is in favour of this legislation. This is the Danish Employers' Association. They're sitting right now at their headquarters licking their mouths. But maybe it's not really any of these three reasons that are the most important. Perhaps in reality it is just that when citizens who are unable to get a job receive money from the public, they get bourgeois evil in the bodily part that is highest when you pick strawberries. Then I would like to say about the so-called economic incentives here that we can only see that they only work when people are actually able to adapt to new conditions. And if you want to help people move, sometimes giving them a boost can actually help. So if you're able to move, you do it. But if for various reasons you cannot move, you fall. The vast majority of recipients of cash benefits are unable to adapt to economic pressure. It just leads to poverty, apathy, upsetness and isolation. Getting a job isn't just an opportunity for everyone. You can be held by many different chains. If you meet two broad-brought bikers on the sidewalk on such a dark night and they yell to you that you have to move, it's getting away. But if you are not able to move, perhaps because you are paralyzed in your legs or tied on your hands and feet, you are beaten up. It is the same now: there is a blue bully bank on its way to the citizens who are at the bottom of society and on the edge of the labour market.
