ParlaMint-DK_20160315231136	After all, we have had the debate on the ceiling for cash aid a great deal of times in this Chamber already, but of course I would like to explain the Conservatives' position on everything. I would like to begin by thanking the rapporteur of the Left for having greeted me earlier, but fortunately I have come back, so I also have the opportunity to give my opinion here in the Chamber. We believe that this is a good reform. We think it is one of the best reforms that has been made in this parliamentary period so far, and we do so because we are basically a party that is concerned with creating wealth in Denmark. We are concerned that we do not have too many people on passive support. We are also concerned about keeping taxes down, and we are concerned about the moral aspect that lies in the fact that there is a reward for those people who go to work rather than being on passive support. These are the three objectives that will be achieved with this cash aid reform. It is achieved that a little more people are going to work; it is achieved that we save some money; and it is achieved from a reasonable point of view that there is a better relationship between what you have, if you are in work, and what you have, if you are on cash aid. Then we are fully aware that we must, of course, also provide people who are on cash benefits with a decent living basis, and we certainly do so with this ceiling on cash benefits. After all, it is a highly nuanced cash aid ceiling, which has different rates depending on how many children you have and what a family situation you are in. But to take one example, we can take this family with two people on cash benefits who have two children. They want almost 22,000 kroner. after taxes a month. And that is what we think is quite a decent basis for life. Of course, it could always be higher if the money grew on the trees. And, of course, if we had no problems with our labour market, it could be even higher. But we think that this level is perfectly decent. There has been some discussion over the last few days about whether this would mean that some people will have to move, and it may well be that it does. However, there is still the possibility that you can have a dwelling of a good standard. Maybe not the accommodation you had when you worked and had a higher income. It may well be that, if you are on cash aid for a long time, you will have to adjust your expenditure to the income that you have and which will be lower than when you were at work. That can't be changed. It cannot be that we can guarantee that all people can stay. If they go from being in work to being on cash benefits, then it would be natural for them to have to adjust their spending in the longer term. All in all, we think that the parties behind this are doing what is responsible. They address one of Denmark's major problems, namely that we have too low growth, that we have too few people at work, that we have too many people on passive support, and that we spend too much money on it. If you look at abroad, you can also see that the level will continue to be very high in Denmark compared to other countries, and we do not think there is anything to be ashamed of with regard to this ceiling for cash aid. We believe that this is a good reform that makes Denmark richer without compromising that there is still a good social safety net for everyone in this country.

ParlaMint-DK_20160315231904	Thank you. With the ceiling on cash benefits, we put an upper hand over how much you can receive overall in cash benefits, special support and housing support. If the total benefits to the person exceed the ceiling, special support and housing support will be reduced. The ceiling does not mean that the cash benefits themselves are cut. With the ceiling on cash benefits, we ensure that it is better to work full-time, part-time or just a few hours a week. It is common sense that there must be a difference between being on public support and supporting oneself, and this will contribute to fewer people being supported by the public and more people becoming part of the working community. For the majority, this is approx. The total reduction in benefits will be less than DKr 2 000. The average reduction in benefits will be approx. 1,450 kr. per month for persons affected by the ceiling on cash benefits. With the ceiling it will be the case that a typical cash aid couple with three children will continue to have approx. 15,000 kroner. available a month after the tax and rent have been paid, or 406,000 DKK. per year in total public benefits, taking into account the average family type calculations. We in the government would like to be the target of this. The ceiling on cash benefits does not change the rates of the cash benefits system. In other words, citizens continue to receive the same allowance as hitherto. In addition, by working even a few hours a week, the individual citizen will be able to create space below the ceiling to receive again all or part of the special support or housing support to which he or she may have lost his or her right because of the ceiling. At the same time, the parties to the agreement have stressed that cash benefits recipients must be given the right help to return to the labour market. Therefore, municipalities must systematically follow up rehabilitation teams on whether a citizen should be visited for example. a sequence of resources or flex jobs, and if a cash benefit recipient is more permanently unable to work, he shall not be in the cash benefits system at all. Finally, the government will ensure that disabled people with special housing needs are exempted from the ceiling. This relates specifically to specific support and housing support for persons with severe reduced mobility and 24-hour care in special housing, as well as housing support for persons with mental or physical disabilities who are searched for and assigned a special accommodation. The draft law also introduces a requirement that those who can work 225 hours a year to maintain full cash benefits. The 225 hours of employment within 12 months can be achieved with 5-6 hours of work per year. week or approx. A month and a half of full-time employment. The rule is designed to ensure that cash benefits recipients are actually available for the labour market. If a cash benefit recipient, who is deemed to be able to obtain employment equivalent to 225 hours within one year, does not meet the employment requirement, the person will be reduced in the benefit. If an unmarried person fails to meet the employment requirement, the reduction in benefits will be either DKK 500 or DKK 1,000. per month, depending on the rate of cash benefits received by the person concerned. A married couple, where one or both of them do not meet the requirement, could be reduced by an amount equivalent to an adult benefit. I believe that the legislative proposal has struck a fair balance in matters where, on the one hand, we strengthen the economic incentive to work while, on the other hand, we ensure that everyone continues to have a reasonable dependency. Both the ceiling rule and the 225-hour rule contain the necessary exceptions to ensure, for example, that: disabled people who have necessary higher housing costs are not affected inappropriately and those who are currently unable to obtain 225 hours of employment within one year are not reduced in their assistance because of the increased availability requirement. I think it is a good thing that we are now creating an increased incentive to take a job with the capacity that a human being now has and that more people will become part of the working community. I would also like to thank you for today's debate.
